Zinc is typically coordinated by residues with lone pairs that can coordinate the metal: His (imidazole nitrogen), Asp (carboxylate), Glu (carboxylate), and sometimes Cys (thiol). Common zinc coordination patterns in enzymes include:
- His/His/His (e.g., carbonic anhydrase)
- His/His/Asp (e.g., many metalloproteases)
- Asp/His/Asp
- Glu/His/Asp
- Cys/Cys/His

The combination His250, Asp262, Asp323 (option A) gives one His and two Asp residues.

The combination Asp262, Glu295, His401 (option C) gives one Asp, one Glu, and one His.

The combination His250, Asp323, His401 (option D) gives two His and one Asp.

For many zinc-dependent enzymes (especially metalloproteases like the &quot;HxE&quot; motif family or related families), common zinc coordination involves combinations of His, Asp, and Glu.

This looks like it could be a meprin or astacin-family metalloprotease. These typically coordinate zinc with His and Asp/Glu residues.

For astacin-family proteases, the zinc is typically coordinated by two His and one Asp (His-Asp-His pattern) or similar combinations.

For meprin (which is an astacin-family member), the zinc binding site typically involves His and Asp residues.

Let me look at the options again:
- Option D: His250, Asp323, His401 - two His and one Asp

This is a common zinc coordination pattern in metalloproteases. The spacing between His250 and Asp323 is about 73 residues, and between Asp323 and His401 is about 78 residues. These distances are consistent with a protein fold that brings distant residues together in the active site.

For astacin-type metalloproteases, the zinc coordination is typically by His, Asp, and His (or similar combinations). The His-Asp-His pattern is very common.

Given the choices, option D (His250, Asp323, His401) with the His-Asp-His coordination pattern is most consistent with astacin-family zinc metalloproteases.

And option C (Asp262, Glu295, His401) which gives Asp-Glu-His.

The His-Asp-His pattern (option D) is the most classic zinc coordination pattern for many metalloprotease families including astacins.

<td valign="top"><strong>Question:</strong> Which reaction best describes the direct catalytic activity of this protein as a subunit of the FGAM synthase complex?<br><br><strong>Choices:</strong><br><br><strong>A.</strong> L-asparagine + H₂O → L-aspartate + NH₄⁺<br><br><strong>B.</strong> L-glutamine + H₂O → L-glutamate + NH₄⁺<br><br><strong>C.</strong> FGAR + L-glutamine + ATP + H₂O → FGAM + L-glutamate + ADP + phosphate<br><br><strong>D.</strong> L-glutamate + NH₄⁺ + ATP → L-glutamine + ADP + phosphate<br><br><strong>Flash-Next response:</strong><br><br><br><br>1. reasoning: The protein sequence shows characteristic features of a glutamine amidotransferase (GAT) class I domain:<br>- The &quot;GGFSYGDYLRPG&quot; motif contains the catalytic cysteine<br>- The &quot;CNG&quot; motif is present<br>- The His residue in &quot;IAHGFGRY&quot; is part of the catalytic triad (Cys-His-Glu)<br><br>This protein is the PurQ subunit from a Gram-positive bacterium (likely *Bacillus subtilis*), which serves as the glutamine amidotransferase component of the FGAM synthase complex (PurL/PurQ). <br><br>The question asks specifically about the **direct catalytic activity of this subunit**. As a GAT domain protein, its direct function is the hydrolysis of glutamine to release ammonia:<br>L-glutamine + H₂O → L-glutamate + NH₄⁺<br><br>The ammonia is then channeled to the PurL subunit for FGAM synthesis.<br><br>Evaluating the options:<br>- A: Asparaginase activity (wrong substrate)<br>- B: Glutamine hydrolysis — this is exactly the GAT domain&#x27;s direct catalytic activity ✓<br>- C: This describes the **overall** reaction of the complete FGAM synthase complex, not the direct activity of this subunit<br>- D: Glutamine synthetase activity (reverse direction, wrong enzyme class)<br><br>The key distinction is between B (direct activity of this subunit) and C (overall activity of the full complex).
